class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1-1] and nums[p2] == nums[p2-1] == nums[p1-2]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p2-1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break
            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1-1] and nums[p2] == nums[p2-1] == nums[p1-2]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p2-1]:

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) <= 2:
            return len(nums)
        i = 2
        for j in range(2, len(nums)):
            if nums[j] != nums[i - 2]:
                nums[i] = nums[j]
                i += 1
        return i

This is LeetCode 80 standard. It keeps at most two duplicates because if nums[j] != nums[i-2], then writing at i is safe. Need verify with examples:

Option 1 standard:
from typing import List
class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        i = 0
        for x in nums:
            if i < 2 or x != nums[i - 2]:
                nums[i] = x
                i += 1
        return i

This is even simpler. It handles len < 2 automatically. If nums empty, i=0 return0. If len1, i=1. If len2, i=2. Good. It uses `nums[i-2]` when i>=2. For i=2, nums[0] exists. Good. This is common.

Option 2:
class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) <= 2:
            return len(nums)
        i = 2
        for j in range(2, len(nums)):
            if nums[j] != nums[i-2]:
                nums[i] = nums[j]
                i += 1
        return i

Which is more efficient? Both O(n). Option 1 uses for x in nums, writes even when duplicate? Actually if i<2 or x != nums[i-2], writes. For duplicate, skip. Good. Option 2 starts i=2, no need handle len<2? If len=0, i=2 invalid? Need guard. Option 1 no guard. But original has guard. Both okay.

```
class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1-1] and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break
            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1
```

Need prove fixed original algorithm. It is essentially:
- p1 is index of last kept element (starting 1).
- p2 is next candidate.
- If last two kept elements are equal and candidate equals that value, skip all remaining elements equal to that value (because sorted, all duplicates of that value contiguous). If p2 reaches end, break.
- Otherwise, copy candidate to p1+1 and advance.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1 - 1] and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break

            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1

Now, if we include import, final code:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1 - 1] and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break

            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1

Need ensure if p2 == len after skip, we break before p1 += 1 and nums[p1] = nums[p2]. Good.

What if p2 == len at top of while? Loop exits, return p1+1. Good.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1 - 1] and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break

            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

Output only the fixed Python code."

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1 - 1] and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break

            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1 - 1] and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break

            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1
```

Yes.

Now, let's consider if the code's skip while `nums[p2] == nums[p1]` might skip values that are equal to p1 but p1 is not the duplicate value? It is. Good.

Let's maybe think if there is any issue with the code if p1 is 1 and p1-1=0, okay. If p1 is 0? no.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def removeDuplicates(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        if len(nums) < 3:
            return len(nums)

        p1, p2 = 1, 2

        while p2 < len(nums):
            if nums[p1] == nums[p1 - 1] and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                while p2 < len(nums) and nums[p2] == nums[p1]:
                    p2 += 1
                if p2 == len(nums):
                    break

            p1 += 1
            nums[p1] = nums[p2]
            p2 += 1

        return p1 + 1
